Note on the regional row: New Jersey needs two, because the regional function is split. ESCNJ and the county educational services commissions are the purchasing channel with no regulatory authority; the 21 County Offices of Education are the regulatory channel with no purchasing role. There is also no verifiable CoSN New Jersey chapter, so the district CTO audience gathers at Techspo in January and at the educational computing cooperative in the spring.

11. Key Notes for Engagement

Last verified: August 2026. WORKING DRAFT for Jeremiah's edit. Research-generated engagement judgment, not settled advice

How to think about New Jersey right now:

  • Open with the cap, not with the state aid number: The hard 2 percent levy cap with only three adjustments, none of them for lost state aid, is the constraint every superintendent and business administrator is living inside. Pair that with the health benefits increase near 34 percent and you have described their year better than any headline aid figure does. This is the state where showing you understand the mechanism, rather than the total, is the credibility test.

  • The budget is approved by a state official, not by voters, and that changes the calendar: For roughly 577 of 590 districts the binding approval is the Executive County Superintendent's, and every one of those 21 offices is currently led by an interim or vacant. Build slack into any procurement timeline that lands near spring budget adoption, and do not assume a relationship at the county level will move anything.

  • Know the current thresholds and ask about the purchasing agent: The bid threshold is $53,000 with a Qualified Purchasing Agent and $39,000 without, effective July 1, 2025, with quotation thresholds of $7,950 and $5,850. The prior figures are still circulating in vendor material and are wrong by nearly $10,000. Asking early whether a district's business administrator holds the designation tells you immediately which deals need a public bid.

  • Do not assume a national cooperative award travels here: New Jersey requires a registered cooperative pricing system with a New Jersey contracting unit as lead agency and a state-assigned identifier on every document. The route that works is national specifications bid by a New Jersey lead agency, which in practice means an ESC award, a state contract through the Division of Purchase and Property, or a district bid. Verify current ESC contract validity given the pending comptroller matter before relying on a specific award.

  • The phone law made school-issued devices the compliant channel, and the state said so in writing: NJDOE's July 2026 guidance states that students may use district-issued technology and sponsored platforms during the school day, and the statute excludes school-provided devices by definition. That is a clean, quotable, educator-first fact: the restriction is on personal devices, and district-managed hardware is how instruction continues. It is also the most useful thing a technology director can tell a nervous board.

  • AI is the open field, and the state has left it open: Two-year-old questions for consideration, no mandate, no recurring funding, and a bill sitting in committee, against a technology leadership community that ran 40-plus AI sessions in January. Helping districts write policy, plan professional learning, and think through responsible implementation is genuinely needed work here, and it puts Dell in the conversation before the state framework exists rather than after.

  • Point people at the facilities grant channel, because almost nobody does: The regular operating district grant program funds at least 40 percent of eligible project costs and lists technology infrastructure upgrades among eligible project types. In a state with a hard levy cap and no ed-tech fund, that is the most reliable path to funded network and infrastructure work, and it is materially under-used. Knowing it exists is a differentiator with a business administrator.

  • 590 districts means coverage is the strategic problem: After Newark there is no dominant district and the median is around 1,500 to 2,000 students. That makes the ESC contract, the state contract, and the two annual gatherings that reach everyone, Workshop in October and Techspo in January, disproportionately valuable. It also means the 21 county vocational districts are worth treating as a distinct segment, since they are institutionally separate buyers with their own CTE funding.

Open Items / To Develop

  • Health benefits vote: The School Employees Health Benefits Commission was scheduled to set active-employee rates on August 19, 2026, with an increase near 34 percent under consideration. This will materially change every district's FY2028 budget capacity. Check the outcome first.

  • ESCNJ co-op status: The January 2026 comptroller directive has no published follow-up and the co-op's bid series has not advanced to the current fiscal year. Confirm current contract validity directly with the commission before routing a deal through it.

  • Funding formula review: The administration's outreach effort with the Hunt Institute convenes a steering committee in fall 2026 with near-term recommendations to the Legislature. Track what it recommends, because it will reset aid distribution.

  • Kids Code litigation: The New Jersey Kids Code was signed August 11, 2026 and no challenge had been filed as of mid-August. Given the pattern in comparable states, re-check this quarterly.

  • AI legislation: Three bills would create district AI policy requirements, AI instruction, or state instructional technology guidelines. All sit in committee. Track whether any moves in the fall session.

  • Facilities grant round: No current-year regular operating district grant round or deadline is posted. Contact the Schools Development Authority directly to confirm the round, the state share for a given district, and how technology infrastructure is scoped in practice.

  • Lakewood intervention: The state's full intervention filing is contested and was still before an administrative law judge as of mid-2026. The outcome will shape how NJDOE uses intervention authority going forward.

  • Association dates: Confirm NJASBO conference dates and the 2027 educational computing cooperative conference before planning travel.

  • Coverage mapping: Map Dell's district relationships against the 21 county offices, against ESC membership, and separately against the 21 county vocational districts, to find both coverage gaps and the segments where a distinct approach is warranted.

NY New York

1. Snapshot

Last verified: August 2026

Leadership & Governance
Governor Kathy Hochul (D), in office since August 2021. On the ballot November 3, 2026
Secretary of Education None. No cabinet education secretary and no governor-appointed state chief. The Board of Regents governs education
Commissioner of Education Dr. Betty A. Rosa, permanent since February 8, 2021. Also President of the University of the State of New York. Confirmed in seat February 2026
State chief selection Appointed by the Board of Regents and serving at their pleasure. The Governor has no appointment or removal power
Board of Regents 17 members elected by the Legislature in joint session, five-year terms, unpaid. Chancellor: Lester W. Young, Jr. Sets regulation, standards, graduation requirements
Local control Strong. Roughly 680 to 700 districts, most fiscally independent with their own taxing authority and a May voter budget vote. The Big 5 city districts are fiscally dependent
Scale
School districts Roughly 680 to 700. 675 were subject to the tax cap for FY beginning July 1, 2026 (excludes the Big 5)
Students / schools ~2.42 million students (2024-25 BEDS); roughly 4,400 to 4,800 public schools
Regional layer 37 BOCES and 12 Regional Information Centers. The Big 5 are statutorily excluded from BOCES membership
Largest districts New York City (~850K, about 35% of state enrollment), Buffalo (~30K), Rochester (~22K), Yonkers, Syracuse (~19K). Largest non-Big-5: Brentwood UFSD, under 18,000
Money & Calendar
Funding formula Foundation Aid (2007, the Campaign for Fiscal Equity response). Weighted for poverty, English learners, disability, sparsity. Revised in FY2026 and FY2027 (see Section 4)
Budget cycle Annual. State fiscal year begins April 1, the regional outlier. Districts still run July 1 to June 30
Current state budget FY2027 enacted May 26-27, 2026, 57 days late. $39B total school aid, $27.4B Foundation Aid, 2% guaranteed minimum increase per district
Legislative session Annual. Convened January 7, 2026; last scheduled session day June 4. Executive Budget due mid-to-late January
District budget rhythm Built January-April, adopted by late April, approved by voters at the polls the third Tuesday in May. May 19, 2026. May 18, 2027. Revote in June if defeated
Property tax cap Levy growth capped at the lesser of 2% or CPI (Ch. 97 of the Laws of 2011). 2026-27 factor: 2.0%, fifth straight year at the ceiling. Override takes 60%
Quick Yes/No
State AI guidance for schools? NO. None as of August 2026, only a March 2024 informational Regents presentation. 30-plus states have acted; New York has not (see Section 2)
Cell phone restriction? YES. Statewide bell-to-bell, all grades, including lunch and passing periods. Education Law 2803 (L. 2025 ch. 56), in force since SY2025-26 (see Section 2)
Screen time action? YES, two platform laws. SAFE for Kids Act (final rules July 28, 2026, compliance January 25, 2027) and the Child Data Protection Act (in force June 20, 2025)
Student data privacy law? YES, among the strictest in the country. Education Law 2-d plus 8 NYCRR Part 121: named Data Protection Officer, NIST CSF alignment, 7-day vendor breach clock (see Section 2)
SDPC / standard NDPA state? YES to both. NYSED bought statewide SDPC membership in November 2023. New York uses the National DPA with NY Exhibits G, J, K, L, administered by the 12 RICs
1:1 / state device program? No statewide mandate. The Smart Schools Bond Act ($2B, voter-approved 2014) funded much of the installed base. No recurring refresh fund
ESA / voucher program? No state program, and one of the firmest non-voucher states in the country. Hochul signaled intent May 8, 2026 to opt into the federal tax-credit scholarship. Not finalized
Collective bargaining Mandatory and strong. Taylor Law (Civil Service Law Article 14). Strikes prohibited. The Triborough Amendment continues expired contract terms, including step increases

2. Hot Topics: The Four Questions of 2026

Last verified: August 2026

These are the issues New York district leaders are answering to their boards right now. Note the asymmetry: the state moved hard and early on phones and privacy, has two platform-side screen time laws in the pipeline, and has done essentially nothing on AI.

ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE | No statewide guidance. Governed by default through Ed Law 2-d.

  • Where it stands: NYSED has issued no statewide P-12 AI guidance as of August 2026. The only state action is a March 11, 2024 informational presentation to the Regents, which named Ed Law 2-d, FERPA, and COPPA as the controlling framework and had no regulatory effect. More than 30 states have published guidance; New York has not, and educators have gone public asking Albany to act.

  • What governs instead: AI vendors are handled as ordinary third-party contractors under Ed Law 2-d: signed DPA, published supplemental information, encryption, seven-day breach clock, and a flat bar on commercial use of student data, which is the operative constraint on model training. NYSED's privacy FAQ was last revised May 7, 2021, so districts are applying a 2020 regulation to 2026 products.

  • Pending, not passed: A9190 (Carroll) would prohibit AI in classrooms before high school, with carve-outs for disability interventions and teacher administrative tasks. S7691 (Gonzalez), an AI literacy grant program, sits in Senate Education. Two adjacent laws do bind: the RAISE Act (signed December 19, 2025, effective January 1, 2027) reaches frontier developers only, while the AI Companion Models law (GBL Article 47, in force November 5, 2025) reaches student-facing chatbots.

  • On the ground: The work is regional. BOCES and RICs are vetting AI tools and running teacher AI professional learning, and NYSCATE is the statewide venue. New York City issued preliminary guidance March 24, 2026 then paused finalization after backlash in June, so even the largest district's policy is unsettled.

Source: NYSED data privacy portal | Regents AI presentation, March 2024 | A9190

SCREEN TIME | Two platform laws. SAFE for Kids rules are final; compliance begins January 2027.

  • SAFE for Kids Act: S7694A, signed June 20, 2024. Bars addictive online platforms from serving algorithmic feeds to users under 18, or notifications to minors between midnight and 6 a.m., without verifiable parental consent. Attorney General rulemaking took two years; final rules landed July 28, 2026 and compliance begins January 25, 2027, so the law is final but not yet enforceable.

  • Child Data Protection Act: General Business Law Article 39-FF, effective June 20, 2025 and in force. Under 13 follows COPPA; ages 13 to 17 require the minor's own informed consent unless processing is strictly necessary. The AG's guidance expressly preserves Ed Law 2-d and FERPA, so 2-d remains the controlling regime for school-directed edtech.

  • Litigation status, unresolved: No suit has been filed against the SAFE for Kids Act as of August 15, 2026, and no injunction exists. NetChoice called the rules unconstitutional in a July 29, 2026 statement but has not filed in New York. Treat a challenge before January 2027 as plausible and open, and do not assume New York follows Virginia's enjoined path.

  • Why districts care: These are platform obligations, not school mandates, so they create no district compliance work. They shape the conversation districts are having with parents, and they arrive alongside school-facing money: FY2027 expanded Teen Mental Health First Aid to all 10th graders statewide and grew school-based mental health clinic satellites past 1,300, up from 872 in 2020.

Source: AG final SAFE for Kids rules | Child Data Protection Act guidance | FY2027 youth mental health package

CELL PHONES / CONNECTED DEVICES | Bell to bell, all grades, in force since September 2025. First enforcement reports due September 1, 2026.

  • The law: Education Law 2803, enacted as Chapter 56 of the Laws of 2025 through the FY2026 budget, signed May 9, 2025, effective August 1, 2025. It rode in the budget rather than moving as a standalone bill, which is why guidance came from the Governor's office and NYSED State Aid rather than a Regents rulemaking. New York is the largest state with a bell-to-bell restriction.

  • Scope is broader than phones: The statute covers internet-enabled devices generally, naming smartphones, tablets, and smartwatches, across all instructional and non-instructional time including homeroom, lunch, recess, and passing periods. Having no lunch or passing-period exception is what separates New York from most classroom-only bans. Exemptions cover educational purpose, medical necessity, emergencies, translation, caregiving, and IEP or 504 accommodations.

  • School-issued devices are exempt: Devices supplied by the district, charter school, or BOCES for an educational purpose are expressly excluded. By removing the personal phone as an instructional fallback, the law increases reliance on district-managed fleets. Read it as a tailwind for managed 1:1 programs, not a headwind.

  • The live deadline: Beginning September 1, 2026, every district, charter, and BOCES must publish an annual website report on the prior year's enforcement, disaggregated by demographic group with a disparities analysis. State funding was $13.5 million one-time at $10.90 per secondary student, a fraction of real cost. Statute bars suspending a student where a phone violation is the only reason.

Source: State implementation resources | Albany Law analysis of Ed Law 2803 | NYSED funding guidance

STUDENT DATA PRIVACY | Ed Law 2-d and Part 121. The strictest vendor regime in the region, and the gate on every deal.

  • The statute: Education Law 2-d, enacted in 2014 after the InBloom collapse, which is why the regime is unusually strict. It covers student PII and, unusually, teacher and principal APPR data, and defines third-party contractor broadly enough to capture free tools and click-wrap agreements. Breach penalties are the greater of $5,000 or $10 per affected individual.

  • The regulation is where the burden sits: 8 NYCRR Part 121, effective January 29, 2020. Every agency must post a Parents Bill of Rights, designate a Data Protection Officer registered with NYSED, adopt a policy aligned to the NIST Cybersecurity Framework, train all staff with data access annually, and publish seven-element supplemental information for every contract. No de minimis exception: one free classroom app triggers the same paperwork as an enterprise SIS. The DPO, not the CIO, is the decision gate.

  • The clocks are aggressive: Vendors must notify the district within seven calendar days of discovering a breach, the term most cannot meet under a standard MSA and the most common negotiation friction point. Districts then notify NYSED's Chief Privacy Officer within 10 days and families within 60. Separately, General Municipal Law Article 19-C (July 26, 2025) requires cyber incident reports to state Homeland Security within 72 hours.

  • The efficient path in: New York layers Exhibits G (the 2-d rider), J (district policy and Parents Bill of Rights), K (vendor security plan), and L (supplemental information) onto the National DPA, managed and funded by the 12 RICs through the RIC One Risk Operations Center. Sign once with a lead district or RIC, post it to the SDPC registry, and other agencies join rather than renegotiate. The Big 5 sit outside RIC membership and need separate agreements.

Source: Education Law 2-d | Part 121 overview | RIC One Risk Operations Center

3. Governance & Decision-Making

Last verified: August 2026

Authority flows differently here than anywhere else in the region. The Legislature elects the Board of Regents in joint session, the Regents appoint the Commissioner, and the Commissioner leads NYSED. The Governor appoints no one in that chain. The Regents and Commissioner control the rules. The Governor and Legislature control the money, and the Executive Budget is constitutionally powerful. Foundation Aid changes and the phone restriction both moved through the budget, not the Regents. Both doors matter and they rarely move together.

  • Leadership is stable, which is unusual this year: Commissioner Betty Rosa (permanent since February 2021) and Chancellor Lester W. Young, Jr. (since January 2021) were both confirmed in seat by a February 27, 2026 NYSED statement. Ceylane Meyers-Ruff was appointed Senior Deputy Commissioner for Education Policy in 2026. Regent Roger Tilles departed in February 2026 after 21 years.

  • November 2026 election, provisional note: Hochul is on the ballot November 3, 2026 against Bruce Blakeman (R), and all 213 legislative seats are up. Treat the fiscal and legislative outlook here as provisional until the new session settles in January 2027. The structural difference from Ohio and Massachusetts matters: a change of administration would not change who runs NYSED. It would change budget posture and the durability of Hochul-branded initiatives.

  • Two different budget audiences: Most districts are fiscally independent and take the budget to a public referendum in May, so the budget audience is the electorate. The Big 5 (New York City, Buffalo, Rochester, Syracuse, Yonkers) are fiscally dependent, have no budget vote, and are appropriated by mayor and city council. NYC mayoral control was extended two years to June 30, 2028 in the FY2027 budget.

  • BOCES is the structure to understand: 37 Boards of Cooperative Educational Services (Education Law 1950-1951) serve every district except the Big 5. Each is led by a District Superintendent who is simultaneously the BOCES chief executive and, by statute, the Commissioner's representative in the region. BOCES Aid reimburses a wealth-adjusted share of purchases made through an approved cooperative service agreement (CoSer) the following year, roughly 21% on services and 40% on administrative and capital, so a CFO asks whether something is BOCES aidable before comparing a direct quote. Nested inside the BOCES are 12 Regional Information Centers running student information systems for more than 95% of districts, state reporting, hosted infrastructure, and the statewide privacy machinery. Statewide platform in New York means the twelve RICs, not NYSED.

  • Legislature: Senate Education is chaired by Sen. Shelley Mayer (D, Westchester), active on Foundation Aid legislation; Assembly Education by Assemblymember Michael Benedetto (D, Bronx). Money runs through Senate Finance (Krueger) and Assembly Ways and Means (Pretlow). The Joint Legislative Budget Hearing on Elementary and Secondary Education, January 29 in 2026, is where everyone testifies. No established K-12 technology champion has emerged, which is an open lane.

Sources: Board of Regents | BOCES primer | RIC One | Conference of Big 5

4. Funding & Budget Rhythm

Last verified: August 2026. FY2027 state budget enacted May 26-27, 2026

  • How the formula works: Foundation Aid, enacted in 2007 to settle the Campaign for Fiscal Equity litigation, is the main unrestricted operating aid. Take a per-pupil foundation amount, adjust by a Regional Cost Index and a Pupil Need Index (poverty, English learners, disability, sparsity), subtract the expected local contribution based on wealth, and the state pays the difference. Save harmless, which guaranteed no district received less than the prior year regardless of enrollment loss, has been the contested element for a decade.

  • The formula is being rewritten, through the budget: The Rockefeller Institute delivered a 314-page study on December 2, 2024. FY2026 adopted its core technical fixes: poverty measured by Small Area Income and Poverty Estimates instead of 2000 Census data, and the English learner weight raised from 0.50 to 0.53. FY2027 added first-ever weights for students experiencing homelessness and foster care and kept the 2% guaranteed minimum increase that functionally replaced pure save harmless.

  • Current budget, and a very late one: FY2027 was enacted May 26-27, 2026, 57 days past the April 1 deadline, at roughly $268 billion. School aid totals $39 billion, up $1.7 billion, with Foundation Aid at $27.4 billion. Also in it: universal pre-K consolidated at roughly $1.6 billion with full-day access required for all eligible four-year-olds by 2028-29, and a five-year delay in the electric school bus mandate to 2032, which frees near-term capital capacity. The lateness is what districts will raise: they adopted their own budgets in April and faced voters on May 19 before the state told them their aid.

  • The tax cap governs everything local: Chapter 97 of the Laws of 2011, permanent since 2019, caps growth in the total levy, not the rate, at the lesser of 2% or CPI, with exclusions for voter-approved capital. The 2026-27 factor is 2.0%, the fifth straight year at the ceiling, and exceeding it requires 60% voter approval. In May 2026, 42 districts tried and 24 budgets were defeated, 17 for missing 60%. A budget defeated twice forces a contingency budget with zero levy growth that excludes equipment purchases and leases entirely, which is the sales-cycle risk here.

  • Where technology money lives: BOCES Aid is the largest structural lever outside the Big 5. The Smart Schools Bond Act ($2 billion, voter-approved 2014) funded connectivity, classroom technology, and security, with 1,911 plans approved as of August 3, 2026, but it is a one-time capital pot, not a refresh fund. The recurring aid ceilings have not moved in a generation: Textbook Aid $58.25 (Ed Law 701), Computer Software Aid $14.98 (751), Instructional Computer Hardware and Technology Equipment Aid $24.20 (753). Building Aid covers capital projects, which is why infrastructure gets bundled into bond referenda. No state device refresh program exists, and the Regents' own 2026-27 request contained no technology line item at all.

Sources: FY2027 enacted budget summary | Rockefeller Foundation Aid study | OSC tax cap announcement | State aid for technology purchases

5. Procurement & Vendor Path

Last verified: August 2026

  • Framework: General Municipal Law 103 governs district and BOCES purchasing: competitive bidding above $20,000 for purchase contracts and above $35,000 for public works, applied to aggregate annual purchases of the same commodity rather than per transaction. Award goes to the lowest responsible bidder, or on a best value basis where adopted. Professional services sit outside 103 and are procured by RFP under board policy.

  • State vehicles: GML 104 lets districts buy directly off Office of General Services centralized contracts without their own bid, and NYSED endorses this path. The IT Umbrella family is the main technology vehicle, awarded on lowest price by expedited request for quote. Two cautions: the distributor-based IT Umbrella (Award 22876) expressly prohibits all cloud products, so SaaS needs a different vehicle, and that award runs only to December 6, 2026.

  • Cooperatives, and a 2026 court decision: Authorized paths are OGS contracts, county contracts, BOCES cooperative bidding and CoSers, inter-district cooperative bidding under GML 119-o, and piggybacking under GML 103(16) on contracts competitively awarded by another government. 103(16) requires an enabling local law, and in Lynch, Inc. v. Board of Education of the Maine-Endwell CSD (Third Department, May 21, 2026) the court held it cannot be used for public works. Goods piggybacking survives, and 103(16) is scheduled to sunset June 30, 2027.

  • National cooperatives are not the clean path they are elsewhere: NYSED's cooperative purchasing guidance does not list Sourcewell, E&I, NCPA, or Omnia at all, and states districts may not piggyback except as specifically authorized. New York is a harder state for national co-ops because 103(16) requires both a local enabling law and a governmental lead agency that bid competitively. In practice the BOCES CoSer is the regional path, and it carries the aid advantage besides.

  • The compliance layer is the real gate: For anything touching student data, Ed Law 2-d and Part 121 come before procurement, not after. The sequence that works: sign the New York DPA with Exhibit G through a RIC or lead district, post it on the SDPC registry so others can join, prepare the supplemental information and Parents Bill of Rights addendum, document NIST CSF alignment and encryption, then start the procurement conversation. Doing procurement first and privacy second is the most common way a deal stalls here.

Sources: GML 103 | NYSED cooperative purchasing | OGS Award 22876 | Lynch v. Maine-Endwell CSD

6. Technology & Digital Learning Posture

Last verified: August 2026

  • Devices: No statewide 1:1 mandate, no device standard, no device requirement. The Smart Schools Bond Act funded the bulk of the current installed base. Refresh is unresolved, with no state program and recurring aid ceilings of $24.20 per pupil for hardware.

  • Assessment infrastructure: The computer-based testing transition for grades 3-8 is complete and mandatory as of spring 2026 for ELA, math, and elementary and intermediate science. Every district had to reach a device-per-tested-student ratio and a network capable of concurrent testing by then. That cuts both ways: the infrastructure exists, and the money for it is already spent. Regents exam CBT status is separate and less settled.

  • Statewide platforms and cybersecurity: There is no statewide learning management system and no statewide content platform; the RICs are the de facto platform layer. On security, Part 121 requires a NIST CSF-aligned data security policy at every agency, publicly posted, with profiles and a gap action plan. General Municipal Law Article 19-C (effective July 26, 2025) adds incident reporting to state Homeland Security within 72 hours and ransom payment reporting within 24, and those reports are FOIL-exempt, so public incident data will be thinner here than elsewhere. NYSED also will not approve Smart Schools plans containing facial recognition.

  • AI capacity is regional, not state-led: No statewide NYSED AI guidance and no identified state-funded K-12 AI professional development. What exists is BOCES-run teacher AI programming, RIC-level vendor vetting, and NYSCATE as the statewide venue. Empire AI, the state's flagship AI investment, is higher education focused. There is an open door and no incumbent program to displace.

  • Framing: New York frames educational technology through a compliance and student wellbeing lens, not an innovation lens: a prescriptive privacy regime, mandatory cyber incident reporting, the nation's largest bell-to-bell phone restriction marketed on behavior outcomes, and a refusal to approve facial recognition. Conspicuously absent are a state instructional technology vision, a state LMS, an AI framework, a device standard, or an edtech innovation grant. The first district question is not whether something transforms learning. It is whether it is 2-d compliant and BOCES aidable.

Sources: NYSED computer-based testing | RIC One privacy and security service | GML Article 19-C reporting

7. Instructional Priorities & Mandates

Last verified: August 2026

  • Literacy, and now math: Back to Basics is a Governor's initiative rather than a Regents one, funded at $10 million to train 20,000 educators in the science of reading. Every district had to certify to NYSED by September 2025 that curriculum, instruction, and professional development align with evidence-based reading practice. FY2027 applied the same playbook to math: NYSED must establish math instructional best practices by January 2027 and districts must review their math curricula by September 2027. That is a live curriculum-adoption driver.

  • Computer science: The Computer Science and Digital Fluency Learning Standards were adopted in December 2020 and full implementation began September 1, 2024, so they are mandatory now. CS is not a graduation requirement; courses count as electives or toward a third math or science unit. The bottleneck is certification: teachers of dedicated CS courses need CS certification or a Statement of Continued Eligibility, so districts are meeting the standards largely through integration.

  • Accountability: The ESSA indicator set changed in 2025-26: attendance replaced chronic absenteeism, student growth returned at the elementary and middle level, and college, career, and civic readiness returned at high school. Final determinations released February 12, 2026 identified 17 schools newly for receivership support with 3 exiting. New York retains a school receivership model, unusual among states.

  • Graduation redesign is the biggest story in the state: Under NY Inspires, New York is adopting a Portrait of a Graduate (formally adopted July 2025, not November 2024 as often reported), shifting credits from seat time toward proficiency, sunsetting the Regents exam graduation requirement, and moving to a single unified diploma. The class of 2027 is the last cohort required to pass Regents exams to graduate, the one-diploma system begins fall 2027, cohorts entering in 2027 and 2028 face new credit requirements including one required CTE credit, and full implementation with a statewide transcript arrives in 2029. Regents exams continue for federal accountability. As of June 2026 NYSED had not released detailed specifications, and principals describe being in limbo. Label as pending.

  • CTE and workforce: One CTE credit becomes a graduation requirement for the 2027 and 2028 cohorts, a dated and material mandate. CTE delivery outside the Big 5 is overwhelmingly a BOCES function through regional CTE centers, which is why the Regents asked for $10.1 million specifically for CTE in non-BOCES districts. Multiple P-TECH grant cohorts reach the end of their terms in 2026 and renewal status is unresolved.

Sources: NY Inspires graduation measures FAQ | Chalkbeat on graduation uncertainty | CS and Digital Fluency standards | Back to Basics

8. Landscape & Pressures

Last verified: August 2026

  • Enrollment is falling, and the epicenter is New York City: Cornell's Program on Applied Demographics found K-12 enrollment fell from roughly 3.1 million to 2.8 million between 2013-14 and 2023-24, with nearly 90% of districts losing students and an aging population as the driver. NYC traditional public enrollment fell 19.1% over that period and accounted for most of the statewide loss in 2023-24, while Long Island was flat. The familiar upstate-decline, downstate-growth framing is out of date. The 2% Foundation Aid minimum increase exists precisely because formula results would otherwise cut aid to shrinking districts. New York districts plan to spend over $37,000 per student, consistently the highest in the nation.

  • Workforce: NYSUT published an educator workforce report in January 2026 focused on teacher preparation. Special education is a documented shortage area with dedicated NYSED personnel shortage projects. A new pressure point arrived with the September 2024 computer science certification requirement, which created a shortage category exactly as the CS standards became mandatory.

  • Facilities: Building Aid reimburses a wealth-adjusted share of approved capital costs through an assumed amortization, and the NYSED Office of Facilities Planning reviews plans and issues permits for all district and BOCES capital work. The fact that matters most: voter-approved capital expenditures are excluded from the tax cap, which is why districts route cabling, electrical, and network work through bond referenda rather than the operating budget.

  • Choice pressure is limited but shifting: New York has no ESA and no voucher program. Charter growth is capped out where it matters: of the statewide cap of 460, 84 charters remain available outside New York City and zero remain in the city, and no cap increase passed in FY2027. The live variable is federal: Hochul signaled intent on May 8, 2026 to opt New York into the new federal tax-credit scholarship program, a significant break from precedent. Status is announced intent, not finalized.

  • Federal climate: New York districts carry substantial federal exposure, with New York City alone receiving roughly $2 billion. Districts spent 2025 and 2026 managing funding threats, including a Senate amendment that would have cut about $170 million from NYC schools before it was defeated. No named state contingency or backfill fund has been identified, and a $268 billion budget enacted 57 days late leaves limited appetite for new reserves.

Sources: Cornell enrollment brief | Empire Center enrollment analysis | NYSED charter fact sheet | Chalkbeat on the federal tax-credit scholarship

9. Organizations & Calendar

10. Key Notes for Engagement

Last verified: August 2026. WORKING DRAFT for Jeremiah's edit. Research-generated engagement judgment, not settled advice

How to think about New York right now:

  • Learn BOCES before anything else: Outside New York City this is a long-tail market: the second largest district is under 31,000 students. District-by-district does not scale, and the state built the answer into its structure. A District Superintendent is both a regional CEO and the Commissioner's statutory representative, which makes that one relationship worth twenty district relationships. BOCES Aid also means a district's net cost through a CoSer can beat a direct quote even at a higher gross price.

  • Ed Law 2-d is not a checkbox, it is the door: The strictest vendor privacy regime in this batch. Every agency has a named Data Protection Officer who is the real decision gate, every contract requires published supplemental information with no de minimis exception, NIST CSF alignment is required by regulation, and the vendor breach clock is seven days. Fluency here distinguishes a serious partner from a vendor who spends eight months discovering the rules.

  • The phone ban is a tailwind, and there is a deadline this month: School-issued devices are expressly exempt, which makes district-managed fleets the sanctioned classroom technology precisely as ESSER-bought devices age out with no state refresh fund. Meanwhile the first annual enforcement reports are due on district websites September 1, 2026. Ask how year one actually went rather than leading with product.

  • The AI vacuum is the clearest opening in the state: No statewide guidance, no model policy, no vetted vendor list, a privacy FAQ last updated in 2021, and a pending Assembly bill that would ban AI in classrooms before high school. Districts are absorbing that risk locally and BOCES are filling the gap regionally. The educator-first move is helping districts and BOCES think through policy, professional learning, and 2-d-compliant implementation while the state is silent.

  • Budget empathy means understanding the May vote: The state budget was 57 days late, so districts faced voters without knowing their aid, and the levy cap has been pinned at 2% for five straight years. A budget defeated twice forces a contingency budget that strips equipment purchases for the year. Track anything depending on a May proposition against the June revote date. The influence calendar: Executive Budget in January, district budget development January to April (when new line items get in or do not), NYSCATE in November, and the NYSCOSS Winter Institute in March.

  • Graduation redesign is the anxiety, and the opening: Every district must build proficiency-based credit tracking, portfolio and capstone assessment, work-based learning documentation, and a statewide transcript by 2029, and as of June 2026 the specifications were not published. This is a listening conversation first, and it matters more to a New York superintendent right now than any technology question.

  • Two New Yorks, two motions: The Big 5 sit outside BOCES and RIC membership entirely: no budget vote, no BOCES aid, no shared DPA, appropriated by city hall. Everything above about the regional motion is inapplicable there. Plan separate motions, and do not mistake a win in one for a pattern in the other.

Open Items / To Develop

  • Track whether NYSED issues any statewide K-12 AI guidance, and watch A9190 (K-8 classroom AI prohibition) in Assembly Education. Both unresolved as of August 2026.

  • Watch for NYSED's detailed graduation measures specifications, expected during 2026-27. Not published as of June 2026.

  • Confirm the Smart Schools Bond Act unallocated remainder (smartschools@nysed.gov), and identify the successor to OGS Award 22876, which expires December 6, 2026.

  • Track SAFE for Kids Act litigation risk ahead of the January 25, 2027 compliance date. No suit filed as of August 15, 2026.

  • Confirm whether New York finalizes its opt-in to the federal tax-credit scholarship program. Intent announced May 8, 2026.

  • Map Dell's existing relationships against the 37 BOCES and 12 RIC regions to find coverage gaps.

  • Schedule a leadership and budget refresh pass for January 2027 after the November 3, 2026 election.

OH Ohio

1. Snapshot

Last verified: August 2026

Leadership & Governance
Governor Mike DeWine (R), term-limited. Open seat on November 3, 2026: Vivek Ramaswamy (R) vs. Dr. Amy Acton (D), polling as a dead heat in mid-2026
Director, Dept. of Education and Workforce Stephen D. Dackin, appointed November 2023, Senate-confirmed December 2023. Verified in seat August 2026. This is the operational chief of Ohio K-12
State chief selection Appointed by the Governor and Senate-confirmed. DEW is a cabinet agency, a 2023 change (see Section 3)
Superintendent of Public Instruction Dr. Philip H. Wagner, selected March 9, 2026, in office late July 2026. Sits under the State Board and handles educator licensure and conduct, not operations
State Board of Education 19 members: 11 elected from education districts plus 8 gubernatorial appointees. President: Paul LaRue. Post-2023 its remit is licensure, educator discipline, and territory transfers
Local control Strong. 606 districts with their own voter-approved taxing authority. No city or county appropriates district budgets, though county budget commissions gained new levy-trimming power in 2026
Scale
School districts 606 traditional districts, plus 339 community (charter) schools and 8 STEM schools
Students / schools 1,588,218 public students (2024-25); about 1.46 million in traditional districts. 2,971 traditional public buildings, roughly 100,600 teachers
Regional layer 51 Educational Service Centers and 16 Information Technology Centers under the Management Council of the Ohio Education Computer Network
Largest districts Columbus City (46,054), Cincinnati (34,390), Cleveland Metropolitan (33,399), Olentangy Local (23,232), South-Western City (21,952). Two Columbus-area suburbs now outrank Toledo and Akron
Money & Calendar
Funding formula Fair School Funding Plan (Cupp-Patterson, 2021): base cost built bottom-up from real district inputs, local share blending property wealth and income, money following the student. Base cost inputs are frozen (see Section 4)
Budget cycle Biennial, odd years. State fiscal year begins July 1. FY2026-27 biennium in effect, enacted as House Bill 96 on June 30, 2025
Current state budget $8.22 billion FY26 and $8.31 billion FY27 in foundation funding to traditional districts. Policy Matters Ohio puts the shortfall against full plan implementation at $2.86 billion
Legislative session Year-round. 136th General Assembly, Republican supermajorities in both chambers above the three-fifths override threshold
District budget rhythm Five-year forecast due November 30 and updated by May 31 (OAC 3301-92-04). Treasurer must certify funds before a contract is valid. Everything else is driven by levy elections
Local approval mechanism Voter-approved levies. Elections in May and November only; August specials are now limited to districts in fiscal emergency (ORC 3501.022)
Quick Yes/No
State AI guidance for schools? YES, and it is a mandate. ORC 3301.24 required a DEW model policy by December 31, 2025 and district adoption by July 1, 2026. Ohio was first in the nation (see Section 2)
Cell phone restriction? YES, a full ban. ORC 3313.753 as amended by HB 96 requires policies prohibiting all cell phone use during the instructional day, compliance deadline January 1, 2026 (see Section 2)
Screen time action? MIXED and unsettled. The 2023 Social Media Parental Notification Act was permanently enjoined in April 2025, then the Sixth Circuit vacated the injunction on June 18, 2026. A rehearing petition is pending (see Section 2)
Student data privacy law? PARTIAL. No SOPIPA-style vendor statute. ORC 3319.321 covers records; Senate Bill 29 (ORC 3319.325 to .327) adds vendor data rules and an unusually strict device monitoring ban (see Section 2)
SDPC / standard NDPA state? Not confirmed. No Ohio SDPC alliance or Ohio-specific NDPA could be verified. DPA practice runs through ITC and Management Council agreement libraries, which is more fragmented
1:1 / state device program? No state 1:1 mandate and no device program. The only recurring state technology money is the K12 Network Subsidy at roughly $2,000 per building per year, tied to E-rate filing
ESA / voucher program? YES, universal. EdChoice Expansion has no income cap, with roughly 143,500 students across the two main programs and about $1 billion a year. It was ruled unconstitutional in June 2025 and is on appeal (see Section 8)
Collective bargaining YES, full public-sector bargaining under ORC Chapter 4117, and teachers may lawfully strike after the impasse process with 10 days written notice

2. Hot Topics: The Four Questions of 2026

Last verified: August 2026

Ohio moved earlier and harder than most states on two of these four: it was the first state to require every district to adopt an AI policy, and it banned phones outright. Its social media law has been in court for two and a half years, and its privacy framework is light except in one very specific place.

ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE | First state in the nation to mandate district AI policies. Deadline already passed.

  • The mandate: ORC 3301.24, enacted by House Bill 96 and effective September 30, 2025, required DEW to develop a model AI policy by December 31, 2025 and required every district, community school, and STEM school to adopt an AI use policy by July 1, 2026. Districts could adopt the state model verbatim or write their own. Ohio was first in the country to impose this.

  • The model policy is published and it is commercially relevant: DEW released it in late December 2025, developed with the Ohio AI in Education Coalition. Its eleven sections include AI Literacy, Data Privacy and Security, Procurement and Evaluation of AI Tools, Ethical Use, and Academic Integrity. The vendor clause requires all third-party AI providers to comply with district standards and with state and federal law for data protection, ethical use, and accessibility.

  • Where compliance actually stands: Unknown. No source tracks district-level adoption and DEW has not published compliance rates or an audit posture, though the deadline passed six weeks before this profile was written. Treat adoption as uneven. The honest discovery question: did you take the state model or write your own, and who owns it now.

  • Also worth knowing: House Bill 594 would require one unit of computer science for graduation, argued explicitly on AI-literacy grounds, and is pending with no hearings held. Several other AI bills (a state AI commission, self-harm guardrails, watermarking) are also pending. District sentiment is skeptical rather than enthusiastic: Ohio Capital Journal ran a piece on August 13, 2026 on schools struggling to figure out what AI is worth buying.

Source: ORC 3301.24 | DEW AI in Ohio's Education | DEW model AI policy

SCREEN TIME | One law, enjoined, then revived on appeal in June 2026. Enforcement status genuinely unsettled.

  • The law: The Social Media Parental Notification Act, enacted in House Bill 33 in 2023, requires social media operators to obtain verifiable parental consent before a user under 16 creates an account. It is a platform obligation, not a school mandate.

  • The litigation, which is the whole story: NetChoice sued in January 2024 and won a preliminary injunction that February. On April 16, 2025 Judge Marbley granted summary judgment for NetChoice and entered a permanent injunction on First Amendment grounds. Ohio appealed, and on June 18, 2026 a divided Sixth Circuit panel upheld the law and vacated the injunction, treating the statute as a parental consent requirement rather than an access restriction.

  • Where it stands today: Contested. NetChoice petitioned for rehearing en banc on July 16, 2026 and that petition is undecided as of August 15, 2026, and the mandate must return to the district court before the order is formally lifted. Do not describe this law as either in force or blocked without that caveat.

  • What else is moving: Senate Bill 167 would shift the mechanism to app-store age verification, designed to survive the problems that sank the 2023 law. Senate Bill 379, Hailey's Law, would require age verification on online gaming sites and add grooming awareness education to required school curriculum, the direct K-12 hook. Both pending, neither enacted.

Source: NetChoice v. Yost | Sixth Circuit decision coverage | SB 167 coverage

CELL PHONES / CONNECTED DEVICES | Full ban during the instructional day. Compliance deadline was January 1, 2026, mid-year.

  • Two steps, not one: House Bill 250 (2024) required every district to adopt a cell phone policy by July 1, 2025, but not necessarily a ban. DeWine called for a full ban in April 2025 and it was folded into House Bill 96 and signed June 30, 2025. The operative citation is ORC 3313.753 as amended, effective September 30, 2025, with parallel provisions for community schools (3314.03) and STEM schools (3326.11).

  • Get the date right: Boards must adopt a policy prohibiting all cell phone use during the instructional day, and the compliance deadline was January 1, 2026, mid-school-year rather than the start of 2025-26. Many districts implemented early and voluntarily, but the legal deadline was January.

  • Scope is effectively bell to bell: The instructional day is defined broadly to include transitions between classes, recess, meal periods, and field trips. The device definition covers any battery or electrically powered device capable of transmitting or receiving communications, which sweeps in smartwatches. Exemptions cover board determination, IEPs, 504 plans, learning purposes, documented health concerns with a physician's statement, and emergency response. Policies must be posted on the district website and in buildings.

  • School-issued devices are not affected, and year one surfaced a real gap: The statute governs student-owned devices. District-issued Chromebooks, laptops, and tablets are untouched, and the learning-purposes exemption preserves them. A March 2026 University of Dayton review found more face-to-face interaction and less digital drama, but also a concrete problem: students could not receive multi-factor authentication codes for college applications, because the codes go to the banned phone. That is a managed-identity problem, not a hardware problem.

Source: ORC 3313.753 | DEW cell phones in Ohio schools | Year-one reporting

STUDENT DATA PRIVACY | Light overall, with one unusually strict rule on device monitoring.

  • What exists: Ohio has no SOPIPA-style statute binding edtech vendors, no parents bill of rights requirement, no per-contract public posting, no mandated security framework, and no Data Protection Officer requirement. ORC 3319.321 is essentially a state restatement of FERPA plus a bar on using directory information for profit-making activity. ORC 3301.0714 bars reporting student names, addresses, and Social Security numbers to the state, using a statewide SSID instead.

  • Senate Bill 29 is the real vendor statute: Enacted October 24, 2024 and codified at ORC 3319.325 to 3319.327. Education records a technology provider creates or maintains are solely district property. No sale, sharing, commercial use, or advertising. Employee access limited to official duties. Breaches disclosed to the district. All records destroyed or returned within 90 days of contract expiration. Districts must notify parents by August 1 annually of contracts with curriculum, testing, or assessment providers, and parents may inspect the full contract.

  • The provision that will surprise a vendor: ORC 3319.327 flatly prohibits districts and technology providers from electronically accessing or monitoring three things on school-issued devices: location tracking, audio or visual capabilities, and student interactions including keystrokes and browsing. Exceptions cover instructional purposes, technical support, exam proctoring, court order, stolen devices, and threats to life or safety, but written parent notice is required, within 72 hours for the warrant, stolen-device, and safety exceptions. This is more restrictive than most states and directly governs monitoring, filtering, and student-safety products.

  • The practical gate is now the AI policy: Ohio's Data Protection Act safe harbor (ORC Chapter 1354) covers businesses and state institutions of higher education but does not extend to K-12 districts. What districts actually ask for is FERPA language, an ITC-supplied or vendor DPA, SSID-only handling, and, since July 1, 2026, alignment with the district's adopted AI policy. Ohio's strongest vendor privacy lever arrived through an AI statute, not a privacy statute.

Source: ORC 3319.326 | ORC 3319.327 device monitoring | ORC 3319.321 student records

3. Governance & Decision-Making

Last verified: August 2026

Ohio restructured its education governance in 2023 and the change is now settled. Authority flows Governor to the Director of the Department of Education and Workforce, a cabinet agency, to 606 districts. House Bill 33 renamed the old Ohio Department of Education, made it a cabinet agency effective October 3, 2023, and moved nearly all academic and operational authority off the elected State Board.

  • Who does what now: DEW owns standards, assessment, report cards, funding administration, EMIS and data, federal programs, community schools and scholarships, career-technical education, and model policies including the AI and cell phone policies. The State Board and the Superintendent of Public Instruction retained educator licensure, conduct and discipline, and territory transfers. Send substantive program questions to DEW.

  • The governance fight is over: State Board members, parents, and the Toledo school board sued in September 2023, but the preliminary injunction was denied that October and on September 16, 2025 the Tenth District affirmed dismissal in Collins v. State on standing grounds, holding the loss of elected board representation was a generalized grievance. No further appeal has been verified. The structure stands.

  • November 2026 election, provisional note: DeWine is term-limited and this is an open seat: Vivek Ramaswamy (R) against Dr. Amy Acton (D), polling as a dead heat in June 2026. Because the DEW Director serves at the Governor's pleasure, a January 2027 change of administration means a probable new Director and a reset of executive priorities, and the incoming governor writes the FY2028-29 executive budget in roughly February 2027. Treat every DEW policy posture here as provisional until then. This is the most consequential leadership question in the batch.

  • HB 920 and the 20-mill floor are the whole game: Districts are fiscally independent with voter-approved taxing authority, but House Bill 920 (1976), implemented through ORC 319.301, reduces the effective rate of every existing voted levy as property values rise so the levy yields roughly the same dollars it did when approved. Operating revenue therefore does not grow with inflation or appreciation. The one exception is the 20-mill floor: reduction factors cannot push current-expense taxes below 20 mills, so districts at the floor do capture reappraisal growth. That is why floor status is the most fought-over question in Ohio school finance, and why passing a levy three years ago does not mean a district is fine.

  • Educational Service Centers: 51 ESCs under ORC Chapter 3311, with service agreements governed by ORC 3313.843. Districts with 16,000 or fewer students must align with an ESC and larger districts nearly all do, but districts may realign every two years and may purchase from any ESC at any time, so ESC agreements are not territorially exclusive. ESCs deliver special education, curriculum, professional development, technology, fiscal services, and cooperative purchasing.

  • Information Technology Centers are the technology channel: 16 ITCs coordinated by the Management Council of the Ohio Education Computer Network, a council of governments under ORC Chapter 167, serving more than 1.8 million students in over 1,000 districts. They host the student information system, run EMIS reporting, provide fiscal software, aggregate network and OARnet transit, deliver cybersecurity tooling, and operate purchasing cooperatives. META Solutions alone serves 475 districts. In Ohio the ITC is frequently the contracting party, the host, the integrator, and the purchasing vehicle at once.

  • Legislature: House Education is chaired by Rep. Sarah Fowler Arthur, Senate Education by Sen. Andrew Brenner. Money runs through House Finance (Rep. Brian Stewart) and Senate Finance (Sen. Jerry Cirino). Senate President Rob McColley led the levy-restriction override and Speaker Matt Huffman drove the property tax package. No legislator has emerged as an education technology champion; the AI work has been executive-branch driven.

Sources: DEW | State Board of Education | OESCA on ESCs | Management Council | Collins v. State

4. Funding & Budget Rhythm

Last verified: August 2026. FY2026-27 biennium in effect

  • How the formula works: The Fair School Funding Plan, the Cupp-Patterson plan enacted in 2021, has three parts. Base cost is built bottom-up from actual reported district inputs rather than a politically chosen per-pupil number, so each district gets its own. Local capacity blends property valuation with resident income. And money follows the student to the school actually attended. It carried a six-year phase-in and a commitment to refresh the input data each biennium.

  • What House Bill 96 did, and why superintendents are angry: Signed June 30, 2025, it continued phase-in years five and six but did not update the base cost inputs, which remain anchored to older cost data. The plan's own WorkGroup describes it as effectively freezing the base cost on 2021 figures, and Policy Matters Ohio calculates a $2.86 billion shortfall against full implementation. Foundation funding to traditional districts is $8.22 billion in FY26 and $8.31 billion in FY27. In place of the phase-in the budget added an Enrollment Growth Supplement and a Performance Supplement that ties dollars to report card star ratings for the first time.

  • The levy structure just got harder, twice: DeWine issued 67 line-item vetoes on HB 96, several protecting district finance. The legislature overrode veto item 66 (House 61-29 on July 21, 2025; Senate 21-11 on October 1, 2025), so from January 1, 2026 districts may no longer place emergency, substitute emergency, combined income and fixed-sum, replacement, or renewal-with-increase levies on the ballot, and existing ones cannot be renewed in those forms. Operating levies, income taxes, permanent improvement levies, bonds, and straight renewals remain. Then five property tax bills signed December 19, 2025 and effective March 18, 2026 tightened the rest: HB 186 caps revenue growth for districts at the 20-mill floor to the GDP deflator, HB 129 makes fixed-sum levies count toward the floor calculation from tax year 2026, HB 309 lets county budget commissions trim voted levy collections annually (not below 20 mills for current operating expense without district consent), and HB 335 caps unvoted inside millage growth. Estimated reductions from HB 129 and HB 335 alone run roughly $282 million in tax year 2026, rising past $500 million by 2028.

  • The May 2026 levy collapse: Only 24 of 66 school levies passed, a 36.4% rate, against 65.5% in November 2025. New levies passed at 24.0% while renewals passed at 75.0%. The structural driver is direct: 75.5% of operating levies on the ballot were new asks because the item 66 override eliminated the renewal forms districts would otherwise have used. Consequences were immediate. Parma authorized $9 million in cuts with layoffs, Streetsboro eliminated JV and freshman sports, Mentor authorized $3.4 million in cuts.

  • Calendar and the 2027 threat: Biennial budget introduced in early February of odd years, signed by June 30. The FY2028-29 process begins in early 2027 under a new governor. District five-year forecasts are due November 30 and updated by May 31, and the treasurer must certify funds before any contract is valid. Levy elections happen in May and November only, since August specials now require fiscal emergency status under ORC 3501.022. Separately, the Committee to Abolish Ohio Property Taxes failed to qualify its amendment for 2026 but is carrying signatures toward November 2027, and every CFO is planning against it.

  • Where technology money lives: The general fund carries recurring software and staffing and is the squeezed pot. Permanent improvement levies (ORC 5705.21, with permanent improvement defined in ORC 5705.01(E) as assets with a five-year-plus useful life) can fund hardware and network infrastructure but generally cannot fund salaries, consumables, or short-lived subscription software. That mismatch is the most common structural obstacle in Ohio edtech deals: a district may have permanent improvement money and no general fund money. Bond issues cover construction with infrastructure embedded, and the state K12 Network Subsidy pays roughly $2,000 per eligible building per year, conditioned on filing E-rate and taking at least 51% of bandwidth from an ITC or OARnet.

Sources: Policy Matters Ohio on HB 96 | Levy elimination override analysis | Property tax package analysis | May 2026 levy results | ORC 319.301

5. Procurement & Vendor Path

Last verified: August 2026

  • Framework, and a favorable exception: ORC 3313.46 governs competitive bidding for districts and ESCs, with the threshold floated through ORC 9.17 at $79,568 for calendar 2026, escalating 3% annually. The exceptions in 3313.46(B) matter: educational materials used in teaching, single-source acquisitions by two-thirds board resolution, energy conservation measures, and computer software and hardware for instructional purposes. That last one exempts instructional computer hardware and software from bidding outright, a meaningfully cleaner path than most states offer. Boards may still impose stricter local policies, so ask for the district's purchasing policy.

  • State vehicles: ORC 125.04(C) authorizes districts to buy from Department of Administrative Services state term schedules without their own bidding. Enrollment in the DAS Cooperative Purchasing Program is free and perpetual with no renewals, more than 2,900 organizations are enrolled, and contracts are searched in OhioBuys. Districts, ESCs, joint vocational districts, and chartered nonpublic schools are all eligible. Covered categories include IT hardware and software.

  • Cooperatives, and which ones actually matter here: ORC 9.48 lets political subdivisions purchase jointly, which is the authority the ESC and ITC cooperatives run on. The dominant vehicles in Ohio K-12 technology are the ITC-run cooperatives and OCEPC, the Ohio Council of Educational Purchasing Consortia, which is Ohio's member of the national AEPA bid and covers 45-plus commodity groups. OMNIA, Sourcewell, TIPS, NCPA, and E&I are legally available and used, but the ITC, ESC, and OCEPC layer is primary here in a way it is not in most states.

  • Registration: OhioBuys is the state supplier portal for state contracts and term schedules, with OAKS as the financial system of record. At district level, each treasurer maintains a vendor file, usually inside the ITC's fiscal system, and a W-9, certificate of insurance, and district data privacy agreement are standard. There is no statewide K-12 vendor registry.

  • Compliance layer: Lighter than New York but not trivial. Senate Bill 29 obligations (ORC 3319.326) attach to any technology provider supplying school-issued devices and creating or maintaining education records: district ownership of records, no commercial use, limited employee access, breach disclosure, and destruction or return within 90 days. ESCs and other public entities are excluded from the technology provider definition, so ESC and ITC relationships are not vendor relationships under the statute. No Ohio SDPC alliance or Ohio-specific NDPA could be verified, so ask the district or ITC rather than assuming a standard form exists.

Sources: ORC 3313.46 | ORC 9.17 threshold | DAS Cooperative Purchasing | OCEPC

6. Technology & Digital Learning Posture

Last verified: August 2026

  • Devices: No statewide 1:1 mandate and no state device program. The dominant device story is post-ESSER refresh: the 2020-2022 wave is now four to six years old and out of warranty, and it is landing at exactly the same moment as the general fund and property tax squeeze. The 2020-2021 emergency device and hotspot programs are concluded, and the Ohio Connectivity Champions service has been retired.

  • Assessment infrastructure: Ohio's State Tests are computer-delivered through a Cambium Assessment portal, covering grades 3-8 ELA and math, science in grades 5 and 8, and six high school end-of-course exams. Cambium's secure browser must be maintained on every testing device, districts pass an annual technology readiness trial, and testing windows are the peak concurrency event on a district network. That pushes districts toward their ITC for bandwidth headroom.

  • Statewide platforms run through the ITCs: There is no state-designated learning management system. What is statewide is the Ohio Education Computer Network: ITC-hosted student information systems (ProgressBook, PowerSchool, Infinite Campus), state-developed fiscal software, EMIS as the mandatory data pipeline, and INFOhio, the still-funded statewide PreK-12 digital library run as a division of the Management Council. Rostering, SSO, and integration conversations run through the ITC, not the district IT director.

  • Cybersecurity: There is no state K-12 cybersecurity mandate and no verified state cyber grant program. Capability lives in the ITC layer: the Management Council coordinates statewide security tooling and standards, offers TechGuard awareness and phishing simulation training at no cost to districts, and publishes alert levels through Ohio K12 Help. Note the countervailing constraint from Section 2: ORC 3319.327 restricts what a district or provider may monitor on a school-issued device, so security and safety tooling must map to a named exception and the parent notice regime.

  • AI capacity building: The DEW model policy is the operative state artifact, and the earlier InnovateOhio AI Toolkit appears superseded. Professional learning is delivered by ESCs and ITCs, with the Ohio ESC Association running an AI Education Project across the 51 ESCs, and DEW publishes AI integration guides for eight content areas. No dedicated state-funded AI professional development appropriation was verified.

  • Framing: Ohio frames technology primarily as compliance and infrastructure, secondarily as instructional, and increasingly as a privacy and wellbeing question. The only recurring K-12 technology money is a network subsidy tied to E-rate filing, the state technology apparatus exists to run reporting, fiscal, and SIS systems, and the named state technology staffer is an E-Rate Coordinator. The Straight A Fund, Ohio's one real innovation fund, has been dormant since roughly 2015. Conversations that lead with instructional transformation land softer here than refresh economics, cyber resilience, ITC integration, and total cost under levy constraints.

Sources: Ohio K12 Help | Management Council cybersecurity | INFOhio | DEW testing

7. Instructional Priorities & Mandates

Last verified: August 2026

  • Literacy is a statutory mandate with an approved-materials gate: ORC 3313.6028, enacted by House Bill 33 and amended effective March 20, 2026, requires science of reading instruction defined as explicit systematic work in phonemic awareness, phonics, vocabulary, fluency, comprehension, and writing. Schools may use only materials from DEW's approved high-quality instructional materials lists, three-cueing is banned by name, and implementation was required from 2024-25 for pre-K through grade 5. ReadOhio anchors the professional learning, and the state named 109 Science of Reading Champion schools in March 2026, up from 48. The Third Grade Reading Guarantee still exists but retention is no longer mandatory and parents may opt out.

  • Math is next: Senate Bill 19, signed June 24, 2026 and effective September 23, 2026, extends the literacy model to mathematics: academic intervention services requirements for reading and math, mathematics improvement and intervention plans, and an emerging high-quality instructional materials expectation in math. It amends ORC 3301.0714, 3302.03, and others. Products touching core reading or math instruction in K-5 need to be on or aligned to DEW's approved lists to be purchasable.

  • Computer science: ORC 3313.6033, effective September 30, 2025, lets any educator holding a valid K-12 license teach a computer science course after completing approved professional development, renewable every five years, with AP-endorsed programs automatically approved. That is Ohio's answer to CS teacher supply. Ohio has adopted CS standards and model curricula. House Bill 594 would make one unit of computer science a graduation requirement and is pending with no hearings held. Whether every district must currently offer a CS course could not be verified against a statute; do not assert it.

  • Accountability now carries dollars: Ohio School Report Cards use a one-to-five star rating in half-star increments across Achievement, Progress, Gap Closing, Graduation, Early Literacy, and College, Career and Workforce Readiness, with separate report card types for districts, career-technical planning districts, and dropout recovery schools. The material 2026 change is financial rather than methodological: HB 96's Performance Supplement pays districts based on star ratings, so report card results now translate directly into state dollars.

  • Graduation and CTE: Graduation requires course credits, demonstrated competency through Algebra I and ELA II end-of-course exams or an alternative pathway, and two diploma seals with at least one state-defined. Career-technical education is a co-equal DEW division, and workforce is literally in the agency's name, which is the clearest signal of state priority. Every district belongs to a career-technical planning district. ORC 3313.6031 now requires districts to grade and rank CTE courses on par with honors courses, a real status-parity change.

Sources: ORC 3313.6028 science of reading | Senate Bill 19 | ORC 3313.6033 computer science | Ohio School Report Cards | DEW career-tech

8. Landscape & Pressures

Last verified: August 2026

  • Enrollment: Statewide enrollment is declining, with roughly 1.46 million students in traditional public schools out of about 1.59 million public students overall. The divergence between urban and Appalachian decline and Columbus-suburb growth is now written into funding policy through the Enrollment Growth Supplement, which exists because districts in Delaware, Union, Licking, and Fairfield counties are growing fast while most of the state shrinks. Olentangy and South-Western now outrank Toledo and Akron.

  • Choice pressure is the dominant budget story: House Bill 33 removed the income cap on EdChoice Expansion in 2023, making every Ohio student eligible on a sliding scale. FY2026 participation runs 42,607 in traditional EdChoice and 100,941 in the Expansion, roughly 171,000 across all strands, at over $1 billion a year. HB 96 added $376 million across the five scholarship programs plus community and STEM facilities funding. Set against a formula whose base cost inputs are frozen, that asymmetry, not the property tax package alone, is the framing Ohio superintendents will use.

  • And the voucher program is in constitutional jeopardy: In Vouchers Hurt Ohio v. State of Ohio, brought by hundreds of public school districts, Judge Jaiza Page of the Franklin County Court of Common Pleas ruled on June 24, 2025 that EdChoice violates the Ohio Constitution's thorough and efficient common schools clause. She stayed the ruling, so the program continues to operate. The Tenth District Court of Appeals heard argument on May 12, 2026 and no decision had issued as of August 15, 2026. Both sides expect the case to reach the Ohio Supreme Court. Because so many districts are plaintiffs, most districts you meet are formally parties.

  • Workforce and facilities: Educator licensure and discipline now sit with the State Board rather than DEW. The clearest verified shortage workaround is the computer science professional development pathway in ORC 3313.6033; substitute teacher flexibility has been extended repeatedly but its current expiration could not be verified. On facilities, the Ohio Facilities Construction Commission administers the Classroom Facilities Assistance Program and publishes the Ohio School Design Manual, which makes it a de facto specifier for cabling, audiovisual, and network design in state-assisted projects.

  • Federal climate: In June 2026 the U.S. Department of Education announced transfers of major functions to other agencies, including IDEA administration to Health and Human Services and the Office for Civil Rights to Justice. A Senate education panel advanced bipartisan legislation to block most of the transfers on August 3, 2026, and the matter is unresolved. Ohio has neither a Returning Education to the States waiver nor Ed-Flex authority, and no state contingency or backfill appropriation was identified.

Sources: EdChoice appeal coverage | Trial court ruling | DEW enrollment data | Federal flexibility coverage

9. Organizations & Calendar

Last verified: August 2026. Confirm dates before travel

OSBA: Ohio School Boards Assn. (www.ohioschoolboards.org) Board members, superintendents, treasurers. The largest single K-12 gathering in Ohio Capital Conference confirmed for 2026, exact dates unconfirmed. Typical timing mid-November, Greater Columbus Convention Center. Regional fall conferences run mid-to-late September
OASBO: Ohio Assn. of School Business Officials (www.oasbo-ohio.org) Treasurers and CFOs, including career-tech and ESC treasurers. The gatekeepers on any purchase 2026-27 professional development calendar is published. Annual conference dates unconfirmed; typical timing spring, Columbus area
Management Council of the Ohio Education Computer Network (www.managementcouncil.org) The 16 ITCs, district technology directors, EMIS coordinators. OCEPC: Ohio Council of Educational Purchasing Consortia (www.ocepc.org) Purchasing consortia serving public districts. Ohio's member of the national AEPA bid Not a conference.
